HERODOTUS,

LATE FELLOW AND TUTOR OF TRINITY COLLEGE, CAMBRIDGE. ΄

“ We are glad to observe that the excellent series of which the volumes before
us form part, still keeps up the high character it has hitherto borne. It is a
gratifying proof that the regrets of some, and the exultation of others, at the sup-
posed extinction of Classical scholarship in England are premature. ¢ Φ #* e
On the whole, we have no hesitation in affirming, that never has Herodotus been
more carefully and efficiently edited —never has his true position as a writer been
more justly determined, his accounts more thoroughly sifted, more fully compared
with those of other writers and authentic monuments, and their real historical
value more clearly exhibited. The excellent tone of feeling running through the
work is in harmony with the lofty style of thought by which it is distinguished.
While the editor has his own opinions, many of which are adverse to those com-
monly received, and holds them with no feeble grasp, he states and supports them
with all the dignified calmness and impartiality of a judicial decision. In this,
as in other respects, the present edition may well serve as a model.”

Atheneum, May 20, 1854.

TRINITY COLLEGE, CAMBRIDGE; AND HEAD MASTER OF KING EDWARD'S

“This handsome volume of the text of Horace is accompanied by an introduc-
tion and argument to each poem, which tell the story of the piece, indicate its
scope and-character, and so far save particular commentary. Yet the annotations
are still numerous, though not too numerous for those who intend a thorough study
of the poet. To the ardent admirers of Horace, Mr. MACLEANE’S criticism some-
times may seem almost depreciatory; but it is searching, reasonable, judicious,
and evidently the result of much thought. There is an independent freshness of
mind about the editor, quite opposite to the usual idea of the commentator. It is
the best edition of Horace extant for English readers.”— Spectator.

=== page 12 ===
PREFACE

ΤῊΙΒ edition of Xenophon’s Cyropmdia pretends to be
no more than is claimed for it in its title, “ A Grammar
School Classic.” It has been drawn up with considerable
care; but the attention bestowed upon it was directed
rather to the end of adapting it to the wants of those
who have to use it, than to the display of scholarship
on the part of the Editor. With this view a very large
proportion of the notes consist of English translations
of short clauses which, from their difficulty, appeared
likely to obstruct the progress of the young student.
Assistance is also occasionally offered in passages which
present no peculiar difficulty, merely with the intent of
suggesting a more clear and elegant rendering than a
boy is likely to devise for himself. Care however has been
taken that such help should not in general supersede that
honest independent drudgery with Lexicon and Grammar
which every scholar must undergo for himself. The ob-
servant master will notice that for this purpose a sentence
has often been remoulded in the translating and thrown
A 2

=== page 13 ===
iv PREFACE.

into a completely different form, such as might fairly be
exhibited in a written version, but would not pass in oral
construing without such scrutiny as would show how far
the student had used or abused the note.

While simplicity in explanation has been the main
object kept in view, so that a few of the notes may per-
haps seem even superfluous, the Editor imagines that
many of them may be worthy the notice of older students
than boys. Itis foolish to fill a school-boy’ 8 copy of a classic
with learned references to works to which he has no ac-
cess, and which he would never look at if he had. It is
perhaps even worse to give him merely puerile aid. That
seems to be the fittest Annotation which 1s a little,
and only a little above the boy’s present capacities;
which may lead him on to take an interest in points
of scholarship without disgusting him with critical
pedantry.

It is a fault in most school notes that they confine
themselves (too drily) to the construction and regi-
men of the sentences as they arise, nauseating the
student with technical phraseology, and oftentimes ex-
plaining a difficulty by an unintelligible grammatical
figure —ignotum per ignotius. The Editor offers no apology
if a note is occasionally expressed in language livelier than
the conventional phraseology of more pretentious works
—or digresses into some historical, social, or even reli-
gious allusion, suggested by the subject-matter, rather
than the bare grammatical analysis of the Greek. Here
and there a note may be found which is intended more
for the Master than the Pupil; venturing, not to ὑπ-

=== page 14 ===
PREFACE. v

struct, but to suggest some point as worthy of more
especial notice.

The text adopted, after much deliberation, is that of
Louis Dindorf, which embodies the results of recent cri-
ticism and is superseding that of Bornemann. From the
explanatory notes of the latter constant aid has been
derived, and frequently acknowledged. Not seldom,
however, his explanation is noticed only to be rejected.
His text has been carefully compared with that of Din-
dorf, and the most important discrepancies pointed out.

The Editor has great pleasure in tendering his warmest
thanks to his friend, the Reverend William Wayte, Fel-
low of King’s College, Cambridge, and Assistant Master
at Eton, who kindly undertook to revise the proofs (both
text and notes), and suggested many very valuable hints
and corrections. To a few of the latter the Editor has
ventured to demur; this he states in order that he alone
may be held responsible for any errors. In the expres-
sion of these obligations the Publishers beg very cor-
dially to concur.

TO THE STUDENT.

Tae Cyropedia was not meant to be regarded as a real
narrative, whether of the education or the career of
Cyrus. It was rather designed to exhibit, under the
guise of a story true in some of its outlines, how a great
and firm empire must be maintained by the animating
vigour and character of a single chief. It was intended
covertly to convey political opinions which no man dared
to avow amid the “free and independent Public’ of
Athens. Xenophon ventures in this form to recommend,
Persian institutions tn name, Spartan policy in fact, to
his countrymen.

It is worth while to read the annexed comments of
Cicero and Ausonius on the purport of the Cyrop=dia.

¢ Cyrus ille a Xenophonte non ad historiee fidem scriptus, sed ad
effigiem justi imperii, cujus summa gravitas ab illo philosopho cum
singulari comitate conjungitur ;—quos quidem libros non sine causa
noster 116 Africanus de manibus ponere non solebat : nullum est enim
preetermissum in iis officium diligentis et moderati imperii.”’— Cic. Ep.
ad Quint. fr, L i. 8.

* Vellem, si rerum natura pateretur, Xenophon Attice, in sevum
nostrum venires, tu, qui ad Cyri virtutes exsequendas votum potius,
quam historiam commodasti, quum diceres, non qualis esset, sed qualis
esse deberet. Si nunc in tempora ista procederes, in nostro Gratiano
cerneres, quod in Cyro tuo non videras, sed optabas.” — Ausonius in
Act. Gratian.

1 g ἐπιμέλονται ὡς ἂν ... εἶεν.
“Τὴ what way they endeavour that
their citizens should be most ex-
cellent.”” Dawes’ Canon is well
known, viz. * iva, ὄφρα, ὡς, ὅπως,
and similar words, take a subjunc-
tive mood when they follow a verb
of present time, and an optative
after a past.” This law has
no exceptions, strictly speaking.
Whenever apparent violations of
it occur the sense must be a modi-
fied one, and not the ordinary one
of mere purpose and eventuality.
Here for example there is a degree
of vagueness and uncertainty im.
plied. “41 endeavour that my
citizens may [pres. and subj.]”
41 endeavoured that they might
[past and opt.].” “I endeavour
that they should (if it may so be)
[present and opt.].”” Cf, § 10,
(where however readings vary)

rival]. κολάζω is more strictly

111. vi. 15, δυνήσεσθαι ποιῆσαι
πείθεσθαί oor. Observe that
ἀπεκρίνατο is followed first by
ὅτι βούλοιτο, and then by an
infin., «νομίζειν. Do not say that
ὅτι is followed by first an opt., and
then an inf. The strict arrange-
ment of the words, with this varied
construction, would be ἀπεκρίνατο
ὅτε μὲν βούλοιτο . . . νομίζειν

1 ἔχειν depends on δίκαιόν ἔστι,

4 ἣν δέ τι apa...‘ But if, as

it does so, because the accusative

the objective) is the proper case

or thought. Hence, in turning

1 τὰς τρίτας σπονδάς. The

4 τὰ 0’ ἄθλα... This vexed
passage presents no particular
difficulty with the reading in the
text. There is a kind of aposio-
pesis (as it is called) after νικώ-

μεθα; i. e. the unpleasant alterna-
tive [“‘we shall become the enemies’
prey’’] is left to the mind rather
than the ear. The words that
follow are the enunciation of a
general principle, applicable to all
cases. “ But as for the rewards of
victory, if we are conquerors (which
we must always suppose, said he,
and endeavour to be), it is plain
that both the enemy and all their
valuables are our own; but if we,
on the other hand, be worsted . . .,
in this case, too, all the property of
the vanquished is always proposed
as a prize for the conquerors.”
If the words τοῦτο... δεῖ are
read after νικώμεθα, they must be
construed, ‘ For this case we must
always mention, and put as sup-
posable.”

whole τάξις of a hundred men were
divided into four λόχοι of twenty-
four men and a leader to each.
Cf. VL iii. 21, ὁ δὲ λόχος ἦν

λόχος had two decads in like
manner; and each of these was
subdivided into parties of five under
a pempadarch. From single file
they ranged themselves first into
four lines, with their lochagi at the
head. These again broke up each
into two; and finally these files
were subdivided again ; so that the
regiment ultimately presented itself
in a body sixteen broad and five
deep; the remaining twenty men
being four lochagi, eight decadarchs,
eight pempadarchs, Each successive
movement had sent officers to the
front of the whole. Of these the
sixteen pempadarchs (eight of
whom were decadarchs as well)
no doubt formed the first rank,

each heading his own column
ranged five deep behind him. The
four lochagi would take up places in
the front of all. The annexed figure
will show the arrangement of a
single λόχος, first, as it came from

1 τἀναντία . . « ὧν,

2 σθημένος. “ Having noticed,
when Tigranes used to hunt with
him, a certain Sophist companying
with him.”” The Sophist was not
necessarily anything but a tutor
[σοφίζω, to make wise]; but he
was habitually inclined to pander
his talents, and for the sake of in-
terest, reputation, or paradox, to
make the worse appear the better
cause; and to train others to do
the ‘like. There is a good note
on the Sophists in Mr. Wayte’s
Protagoras, 311 E, where he gives
definitions of Συφιστῆς from Xeno-
phon [Mem. I. vi. 13], Plato, and
Aristotle; and remarks, ‘ On the
one hand, the Sophist belongs as
muck to a recognized profession
as the statuary or poet; on the
other hand, that profession is not
an honorable one. . . . May we not

suppose that, as professors of
rhetoric, the Sophists enjoyed a
large share of popularity, qualified
bya large, perhaps an equal, amount
of adverse public opinion ?”’
3 Here follows an amusing little
piece of * sophistry ’’ and fencing.
igranes argues, that if Cyrus
approves of bis father’s course, he
may think his example and advice
good to follow; but if Cyrus thinks
him a guilty man, he will hardly
wish to follow his example. In
either case, then, he will be loth
to kill him. Instead of allowing
himself to be caught in the horns
of this dilemma, Cyrus takes up
the latter position, and endeavours
to establish that the best way to
shun the sinner’s example is to do
right; but that it is right to
punish the wrongdoer; therefore,
according to Tigranes’ advice, Cyrus
must kill his father. Tigranes then
leavesthe question of abstract right,
and has recourse to the argument
from expediency.

foray and plundering expeditions

1 ὅπως αὖ... κατίδωσιν. We
ought here to have the optative.
Matthise thus explains or rather
comments on the breach of rule: —
¢¢ The Greeks so readily pass from
oblique to direct narration, and use
the very tenses and moods which
the supposed speaker actually used,
that we cannot be surprised at
their occasionally employing the
subj. for the opt. after ὡς, iva,
and the like.” In order to show
you observe the deviation from the
ordinary rule, do not construe * in

order that they migh! observe ”’
&c. (as you would the opt.), but
rather “with a view to observ-
ing” &c.

2 συμπράττοιεν.
in concert with him,”

3 κελεύω... “I ask’’ or “ urge;”
but not ““ command.”

4 ὁπόσα συι προχωρεῖ. ‘As
much as you have forthcoming.’

8 καλῶς βεβουλεῦσθαι. *¢ That
you were well advised in doing
him a favor.”

There is no great distinction be-
tween the significations of these
two prepositions so employed. A
man is said to fight περὶ that which
he is thinking of, and so for ifs
sake; while he fights ὑπέρ, on be-
Aalf of the side he espouses and
serves. The Russian fights ὑπέρ,
the Englishman περί. As one
would expect from their high and
free sentiments, Greek writers em-

that they who desire victory should
oppose (ἐναντία τάττειν) to the
foe in their flight the sightless, de-
fenceless, helpless parts of their
persons.” Omit ταῦτα, or read
πάντα in its stead. τὸ belongs
not to κρατεῖν (which depends on
βουλ.), but to all that follows.

Ca. II. Cyrus enters into nego-
tiations with certain legates of the
Hyrcanii, at their own request.
Under their guidance he surprises
and routs the enemy’s camp. He
then makes further dispositions
conducive to the comfort of his
own army and their allies.

in fact, two,”’ i.e. “and in par-
ticular, at that time.” There was
a division of the Lacedemonian
army which was in like manner
always exposed to the most dan-
gerous service. It was composed

8. ἃ τ᾽’ ἱποίησαν. Here ἃ re
is to be regarded as equivalent to
one single word. In the older use
of the Greek language re was not
employed as a conjunction merely,
indicating that something mesre
was coming afterwards, with καὶ
or another τε, or linking the word
before it to preceding words, like
the algebraic 4-. Some of its
usages would appear to be quite
archaic, belonging to a most primi.
tive stage of the language, in which
it answered a somewhat pleonastic
purpose of filling up a sentence, or
helping words into combination

clause. So ὥστε, ὅστε, are, and
many other forms came to be idio-
matic, as in Latin quisque, gui-
cungue, and the like. We must
not endeavour to displace the par-
ticle from its place here, in order
to couple διηγοῦντο with καὶ ἐμε-
γαληγόρουν.

But he is not quite accurate in
asserting that “ ὡς ἐπηρώτων is
exactly the same as τὸ ἐπερωτῆ-
gar” It is in gense, but not in
construction ; just as in the pas-
sage adduced from Thucydides,
ὡς λέγουσι * signifies exactly the
same as τὸ λέγειν : but λεγόν-
τῶν should not be made to depend
on ὡς λέγουσι, that being the
genitive to which is referred the
whole ὑπενοεῖτε ὡς λέγουσι, not
merely the part ὡς λέγουσι.

4 ὡς ἔσκωπτον. “ον they
indulged in such banter as was
more pleasant than not,’”’ as we
may translate into our own idiom.
(After μὴ, σκωφθῆναι must be
understood.)

5 ὕβρεως. Personality,” or
perhaps “‘insult;’’ it seems to bave
consisted of * harmless person-

ity.

1 σκηνοῦν. The proper dis-
tinction between σκηνόω and σκη-
viw is that between “setting up a
tent’’ and “living in a tent,” ¢ en-
camping’’ and * being encamped.”
This is however not very strictly
adhered to. Both verbs are used
in II. i. 25, where we have an ac-
count of Cyrus’ camp arrange-
ments, and the principle on which
they were based.

3 rerpdpprpoy. The ancients

e four horses, not as we
usually do, two leaders and two

wheelers, but all abreast. At first
each pair were divided by a pole,
and all four were yoked together.
Subsequently there was only one
pole, dividing the two inner horses ;
the outer ones being attached by
loose traces, more for show than
work. These last were called παρά-
σειροι or σειραφόροι. The inner
pair were ζύγιοι. In the arrange-
ment mentioned in the text the
older method was adopted, only
with twice the number of horses"
(and poles) attached.

and &c.” διαθρύπτω is “ to break
in sunder,” but in passive ‘‘ to be
enervated,’”’ and so in middle “ to
give oneself airs.” Thus in Theocr.

herself up, giving herself airs and
attitudes.” [L. and 8.]

CH. III. The lamentable mourn-
ing and suicide of Panthea over
her deceased husband. Cyrus
shows her every mark of sym-
pathy ; and when consolation is
unavailing, orders all due sepul-
chral honors to the departed.

1 ὅπῃ καιρὸς εἴη. “ Where
there should be fitting oppor-
tunity.” Oy may retain its pro-
per signification, of place.

3 ἀπεκρίνατο ὅτι Ὦ. J. Bornem.
points ἀπεκρίνατο, ὅτι, w—and re-

marks ¢ supplendum οὐδαμοῦ φαί-
ψεται, ὅτι.᾽" But thisis quite wrong,
if it is thereby intended that ὅτε
(in the text) means ‘‘ because,”’ or
is any part of the reply. It is
used (as constantly) to introduce
a speech, and belongs closely to
ἀπεκρίνατο. We only use “ said
—that”’ when the speech is narrated
in Or. obliqua, but in Greek ὅτε
may preface a reply in Or. recta :
as though we might say ‘he re-
plied that ¢ O Master διο.᾽ "

1 ἐξέπλησε. ‘There is another

Cr. V. The siege and fall of
Babylon. Cyrus concerts some
needful regulations with regard to
his appearance in public; a body
guard ; a city police ; the discipline
of the better classes.

Σ περὶ yap πολὺ τεῖχος κυκλ.
Under Nebuchadnezzar, about 50
years before this time, Babylon
‘had reached the summit of its
magnificence. It was in the form
of a square, each side 12 miles in

length. The walls were made of
burnt brick, 50 cubits thick and
200 high. The Euphrates divided
the city into two equal parts. The
Temple of Belus and the Hanging
Gardens were the most celebrated
of the structures of Babylon. Their
fame survives, while nothing now
marks the site of the city they
adorned but huge mounds of earth
and brick.

The construction κυκλουμένους
. + . γενέσθαι τὴν ¢. deserves no-
tice. It commences with the par-
ticiple formed as though it were to
conclude τὴν φάλαγγα ἔχειν, but
the construction changes and the
accusative κυκλ. is left absolute.

1 ὅ,τι αὑτῷ... χρήσεται. This

αὑτοῦ, dependent in that case on
ix" ἀγαθῷ. * Cyrus will never be

3 συνδόξαν. “It having ap-
proved itself to all &c.”” For these

accusatives absolute of the parti-
ciple, 6. g. ἐξόν, τυχόν, ὑπάρχον,
δεδογμένον, see Arnold’s Gr. Gr.
1140, 1141. Matthiee considers
them nominatives absolute. Δόξαν
ἡμῖν ταῦτα (Plato, Protag. 314 C)
is a curious instance of a plural sub-
ject with singular participle, arising
from the idiomatic frequency of
such a word as δόξαν causing it to
pass into an independent word, of
which the grammatical exigencies
are forgotten.

1 ἐπείπερ ... εἶναι. Borne-
mann explains that this clause
should properly follow εἰ αὐτός.
“ He considered that he should
incite . . . if he should endeavour
(inasmuch as he did consider he
was their rightful leader) to show
himself &c.” It would have been
to no purpose to endeavour this,
unless he felt he had a title to
command. I think the sentence is
rather a condensation of two argu-
ments—* He thought he should
incite them &c. if he endea-
voured ;”’ and it was ‘‘ inasmuch as
he thought he was their rightful
leader if [and only if] he should
strive to &c.”” We may translate
‘he hoped most easily to incite
them to high and noble deeds (as

also he considered his own title to
rest) in his endeavouring &c.”

! καὶ νῦν ἔτι ἔχουσιν. It can
scarcely escape observation that
the frequent recurrence of this re-
mark (since Book VII. i. 45, where
it first occurs) is due to the conser-
vative tendency of Xenophon’s own
principles. His object in composing
the Cyropadia was to set favorably
before his countrymen those Spar-
tan principles of government which
he knew to contain more solid
worth and more elements of en-
durance than their own. This it
was his pleasure or his policy to
do under the guise of an account
of Persian government and the
permanence of Persian institutions.

4 ἀνεζεύγνυ. The primary
meaning is ‘to harness again ;᾽ ἢ
but the verb is used (as here) ab-
solutely ‘to move off,’ casira
movere. Thus Thucyd. viii. 108,
ἀναζεύξας ἤλαυνεν ἐπὶ τῆς Ἴω-
νίας. In this case ἀνὰ has no
lon its sense of iteration,
“ again,” but (since repetition is
often reversal) has the significa-
tion of * undoing,” * unyoking,’
and so ‘starting off ;"’ in French
laisser aller.

3 ἐπὶ τὰ τεταγμένα ἄγειν.
There is some difficulty about this
passage, It is explained by Borne-
wann, ‘All the porters come to-

gether to the baggage which is
ready set in order and arranged to
carry.” It appears however ex-
ceedingly awkward to employ ἄγειν
in this manner. Bornemann con-
siders it as dependent on ἔρχονται,
which is too distant to govern it.
It might perhaps be regarded as
ruled by the sum total of the pre-
ceding part of the sentence ; but
in that case wore ἄγειν or row
ἄγειν would be more natural. I
should prefer to construe ‘they
all come together to the [beasts]
appointed to carry the goods,’’ un-
derstanding ὑποζύγια. And this
seems the more easy, because ἐπὶ
ra occurs with that noun ex-
pressed just before, and with it
implied immediately after.

ἐμπίπτειν, i. 6. ὅτι οἱ ἐπιτιθέμενοι

ὥσπερ εἰς ἐνέδραν, as Bornemann

posed that ὥσπερ ἂν ἐμπίπτειν is
strictly good Greek; nor must we
have recourse to any such fiction
as that it is put for any thing else.
The fact is that ὥσπερ ἂν and,
more commonly, ὥσπερ ἂν εἶ, come
. to be used as a single word, so
that dv is merged and ceases to
exact its ordinary constructions.
Mr. Wayte on Plato, Protag. 311 B
(and other passages there cited)
explains such passages as elliptical;

which perhaps comes to much the
same thing, the ellipse becoming
at length so familiar that it is not
(even mentally) noticed. After
ἡγεῖτο we must supply wore.
“ With sach disposition of his
troops he marched onward, [so
that] if any should attack, by

just as into an ambush.”

Among the Greeks and Romans

parents had an absolute authority
over the matrimonial arrangements
of their children. Xenophon con-
tinually attributes Greek customs
and institutions to the Persians;
we have no independent authority
for informing us of the Oriental
custom in this particular.

Cu. VII. Cyrus is admonished
by a vision of his approaching dis-
solution. His parting speech to
his sons and friends, containing
injunctions for their conduct, and
magnificent anticipations of his
own immortality.
1 τοῦ αἰῶνος προκεχωρηκότος.
Ctesias and others make Cyrus die
at the age of seventy. Hdts. (i.
214) assigns to him a reign of
twenty-nine years. The following
ordinarily received chronology is
taken from Talboys’ Oxford Ta-
blés—
cyrus. 569—5629 s.c.

669. Cyrus, having defeated Asty-
ages at Pasargada, overthrows
the kingdom of the Medes
and founds the Persian em-
pire ; comprising Media, Per-
gia, Assyria, Babylonia, Asia
Minor, Syria, Pheenicia, and
Palestine. Residences; Ba-
bylon, Susa, Ecbatana. Bu-
rial place; Persepolis.

546. Victory over Croesus, near
Sardis, after which Cyrus adds
the Lydian empire and all
Asia Minor to his dominions.

The Persians become more
polished by the introduction
of the religion of Zoroaster,
which is adopted by the no-
bler clans, particularly by the
Pasargade. The vanquished
countries are secured by large
standing armies. Tribute ex-
acted. Some nations are trans-
planted, and others weakened
by being compelled to adopt
a luxurious mode of living.

538. Babylon and the provinces
of Babylonia captured by Cy-
rus. The Phcenician states
subjugated. The Persian em-
pire extends from the Medi-
terranean to the Indus and
Ozxus.

. Cyrus killed in a campaign

against the Massagetee. He

is succeeded by bis eldest

addresses this remark to Cambyses,
in order to induce him to show
himself worthy of his brother's
allegiance and affection. For, he
argues, if Ae did not honor him, it
was scarcely probable others would
freely pay him respect.

gada, by many ancients who pro-

Ca. VIII. An Epilogue; set-
ting forth the disastrous effects
which ensued on Cyrus’ decease
and the maladministration of his
SUCOessOrs.

It is difficult to believe that this
concluding chapter is genuine, or
at any rate that Xenophon in-
tended it to close his work. It
contains several inelegancies, anti-
theses which savour of pedantry
rather than Xenophon’s simplicity,
and its matter seems foreign to
the general scope of the book. At

all events it. is an inapt sequel to
the magnificent utterances of the
dying hero, and constitutes a very
painful anticlimax.

varo. The second μὲν belongs to
the subordinate antitheses, North,
South, East, and West, and is re-
sponded to by πρὸς ἄρκτον δέ.
